Even when my husband’s being sweet, or we’re cooking with the family—which used to be something I really looked forward to—I’m there, I’m doing the chopping or stirring or whatever, but it’s like I’m just going through the motions. \n\nIt reminds me of when the windows in our kitchen get that greasy haze on them and you don’t notice until the sun hits it—sorry, that’s probably not the point. But yes, I do feel down a lot of the time, just kind of muted.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over three years, I think. About three years and three months, if I’m counting right. It started around when they moved me into that higher-responsibility role at work—mentoring the newer adjusters and helping oversee the new workflow systems.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the change, you know, because everyone was confused by the new system and asking me questions all day… but it just never really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I still do the things, like cooking with my family or sitting with my husband when he’s being kind and trying to make things nice, but it’s like I’m not really… there the same way.\n\nMost days it feels like there’s this gray film over everything. I’ll be chopping vegetables or stirring something, and I know I used to enjoy that—especially when everyone was in the kitchen talking over each other—but now I’m mostly just going through the motions. Like the activity is happening, but the good feeling doesn’t really reach me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s frustrating, because it doesn’t seem like I’ve done enough to feel that worn out.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. It’s not always like crying or anything obvious, it’s more like most days there’s this gray film over everything. Even when my husband’s being sweet, or we’re cooking with the family—which used to be something I really looked forward to—I’m there, I’m doing the chopping or stirring or whatever, but it’s like I’m just going through the motions. \n\nIt reminds me of when the windows in our kitchen get that greasy haze on them and you don’t notice until the sun hits it—sorry, that’s probably not the point. But yes, I do feel down a lot of the time, just kind of muted.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over three years, I think. I still do the things, like cooking with my family or sitting with my husband when he’s being kind and trying to make things nice, but it’s like I’m not really… there the same way.\n\nMost days it feels like there’s this gray film over everything. I’ve had better days here and there, like if something distracts me or the house is busy, but not really a stretch where I felt like myself again.\n\nIt started around… a little over three years ago. Maybe three years and three months now? It was when I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work, mentoring the adjusters and helping oversee those new workflow systems. I remember because everyone kept saying it was a good opportunity, and I was trying to be grateful, but that’s when this tired, sad feeling really settled in and just… stayed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant differences in how you feel at different times of the year?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern with seasons, like winter being worse or summer being better. Sometimes I’ll think, oh, maybe it’s because it’s been gray outside, but then it’ll be sunny and I’ll still feel about the same.\n\nI don’t track it very well, honestly. I get caught up in work and the house and whatever’s going on with everyone else, and before I know it months have passed. So… no, I don’t think there’s a big difference by time of year, at least not one I can point to.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave your symptoms gotten worse over the past three years?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 55 / claims_adjuster_appraiser_examiner_or_investigator / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on for about 3 years and 3 months, since I was moved into the higher-responsibility role mentoring adjusters and overseeing the new workflow systems.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d say it got sharply worse… it’s more like it settled in and stayed there. It’s been about three years and three months now, since I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work, mentoring the adjusters and helping with those new workflow systems.

At first I kept thinking, well, this is just a stressful stretch, and you know how offices are when they change systems—everyone’s asking questions, nothing works the way it’s supposed to, and suddenly you’re the person people come to. But the feeling didn’t really lift after that. It’s just been going on since then.

It’s not always like crying or anything obvious, it’s more like most days there’s this gray film over everything. Even when my husband’s being sweet, or we’re cooking with the family—which used to be something I really looked forward to—I’m there, I’m doing the chopping or stirring or whatever, but it’s like I’m just going through the motions. \n\nIt reminds me of when the windows in our kitchen get that greasy haze on them and you don’t notice until the sun hits it—sorry, that’s probably not the point. But yes, I do feel down a lot of the time, just kind of muted.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over three years, I think. It started around when they moved me into that higher-responsibility role at work—mentoring the newer adjusters and helping oversee the new workflow systems.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the change, you know, because everyone was confused by the new system and asking me questions all day… but it just never really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I still do the things, like cooking with my family or sitting with my husband when he’s being kind and trying to make things nice, but it’s like I’m not really… there the same way.\n\nMost days it feels like there’s this gray film over everything. I’ll be chopping vegetables or stirring something, and I know I used to enjoy that—especially when everyone was in the kitchen talking over each other—but now I’m mostly just going through the motions. Like the activity is happening, but the good feeling doesn’t really reach me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I mean… there are moments, I guess. Like if we’re cooking as a family, I can still recognize that it’s supposed to be nice, and my husband will be kind or make some little joke, and I’ll think, “This should feel good.”\n\nBut most days it’s like there’s that gray film over everything. I’m doing it—chopping, stirring, smiling at the right times—but the enjoyment doesn’t really land the way it used to. I still do the things, like cooking with my family or sitting with my husband when he’s being kind and trying to make things nice, but it’s like I’m not really… there the same way.\n\nMost days it feels like there’s this gray film over everything. I’ll be chopping vegetables or stirring something, and I know I used to enjoy that—especially when everyone was in the kitchen talking over each other—but now I’m mostly just going through the motions. Like the activity is happening, but the good feeling doesn’t really reach me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I mean… there are moments, I guess. Like if we’re cooking as a family, I can still recognize that it’s supposed to be nice, and my husband will be kind or make some little joke, and I’ll think, “This should feel good.”\n\nBut most days it’s like there’s that gray film over everything. I’m doing it—chopping, stirring, smiling at the right times—but the enjoyment doesn’t really land the way it used to. It’s hard to say I really take pleasure in it, even when I want to.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities where you feel a sense of pleasure or enjoyment that is not diminished?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really, no. I keep trying to think of one, like maybe cooking with everyone, because that used to be such a big thing in our house—my husband would come in and be sweet, or somebody would be laughing about something, and I’d be standing there with the spoon in my hand thinking, this is nice, I should feel nice.\n\nBut most days it’s still like there’s that gray film over everything. I can do it, I can go through the motions and be there with them, but the enjoyment just doesn’t come through the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now feel completely uninterested in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… cooking is probably the main one. It used to be something I actually looked forward to, especially when everybody was around and the kitchen was busy. My husband would come in and be kind, or try to make things feel nice, and I’d usually feel some warmth from that.\n\nNow I still do it, but most days it’s like there’s this gray film over everything. I’m chopping and stirring and making conversation, but I’m not really feeling the enjoyment I used to. I guess I haven’t stopped doing it completely, but inside it feels like I’m just going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling of tiredness and sadness been continuous over the past three years, or have you had periods where you felt better?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty continuous, I think. I’ve had better days here and there, like if something distracts me or the house is busy, but not really a stretch where I felt like myself again.\n\nIt started around… a little over three years ago. Maybe three years and three months now? It was when I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work, mentoring the adjusters and helping oversee those new workflow systems. I remember because everyone kept saying it was a good opportunity, and I was trying to be grateful, but that’s when this tired, sad feeling really settled in and just… stayed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant differences in how you feel at different times of the year?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern with seasons, like winter being worse or summer being better. Sometimes I’ll think, oh, maybe it’s because it’s been gray outside, but then it’ll be sunny and I’ll still feel about the same.\n\nI don’t track it very well, honestly. I get caught up in work and the house and whatever’s going on with everyone else, and before I know it months have passed. So… no, I don’t think there’s a big difference by time of year, at least not one I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms gotten worse over the past three years?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it got sharply worse… it’s more like it settled in and stayed there. It’s been about three years and three months now, since I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work, mentoring the adjusters and helping with those new workflow systems.\n\nAt first I kept thinking, well, this is just a stressful stretch, and you know how offices are when they change systems—everyone’s asking questions, nothing works the way it’s supposed to, and suddenly you’re the person people come to. But the feeling didn’t really lift after that. It’s just been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in how you've been feeling over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Over the past few months… I don’t think there’s been a big change, really. It’s kind of the same thing that’s been going on for about three years and three months now, since I got moved into that higher-responsibility position at work.\n\nThat was when I started mentoring the adjusters and helping oversee those new workflow systems—which, honestly, those systems were supposed to make things easier, but you know how that goes. Everybody has questions, and somehow you become the person who’s expected to know the answer. But anyway… no, the last few months haven’t felt noticeably different. It’s just sort of continued.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods, even if they were brief, where you felt like your old self for at least a couple of months?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might have a decent week here or there, or a weekend where I’m distracted by something, but not a whole couple of months where I’d say I felt like my old self.\n\nIt’s been pretty steady since that work change about three years and three months ago—when they put me in that higher-responsibility role, mentoring the adjusters and dealing with those new workflow systems. And those systems… honestly, they were a whole thing. But no, I can’t point to a couple-month stretch where it really lifted.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I keep trying to think of one, like maybe cooking with everyone, because that used to be such a big thing in our house—my husband would come in and be sweet, or somebody would be laughing about something, and I’d be standing there with the spoon in my hand thinking, this is nice, I should feel nice.\n\nBut most days it’s still like there’s that gray film over everything. I can do it, I can go through the motions and be there with them, but the enjoyment just doesn’t come through the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now feel completely uninterested in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… cooking is probably the main one. It used to be something I actually looked forward to, especially when everybody was around and the kitchen was busy. My husband would come in and be kind, or try to make things feel nice, and I’d usually feel some warmth from that.\n\nNow I still do it, but most days it’s like there’s this gray film over everything. I’m chopping and stirring and making conversation, but I’m not really feeling the enjoyment I used to. I guess I haven’t stopped doing it completely, but inside it feels like I’m just going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling of tiredness and sadness been continuous over the past three years, or have you had periods where you felt better?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty continuous, I think. I’ve had better days here and there, like if something distracts me or the house is busy, but not really a stretch where I felt like myself again.\n\nIt started around… a little over three years ago. Maybe three years and three months now? It was when I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work, mentoring the adjusters and helping oversee those new workflow systems. I remember because everyone kept saying it was a good opportunity, and I was trying to be grateful, but that’s when this tired, sad feeling really settled in and just… stayed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant differences in how you feel at different times of the year?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern with seasons, like winter being worse or summer being better. Sometimes I’ll think, oh, maybe it’s because it’s been gray outside, but then it’ll be sunny and I’ll still feel about the same.\n\nI don’t track it very well, honestly. I get caught up in work and the house and whatever’s going on with everyone else, and before I know it months have passed. So… no, I don’t think there’s a big difference by time of year, at least not one I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms gotten worse over the past three years?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it got sharply worse… it’s more like it settled in and stayed there. It’s been about three years and three months now, since I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work, mentoring the adjusters and helping with those new workflow systems.\n\nAt first I kept thinking, well, this is just a stressful stretch, and you know how offices are when they change systems—everyone’s asking questions, nothing works the way it’s supposed to, and suddenly you’re the person people come to. It’s just been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in how you've been feeling over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Over the past few months… I don’t think there’s been a big change, really. Everybody has questions, and somehow you become the person who’s expected to know the answer. But anyway… no, the last few months haven’t felt noticeably different. I can do it, I can go through the motions and be there with them, but the enjoyment just doesn’t come through the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now feel completely uninterested in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… cooking is probably the main one. It used to be something I actually looked forward to, especially when everybody was around and the kitchen was busy. My husband would come in and be kind, or try to make things feel nice, and I’d usually feel some warmth from that.\n\nNow I still do it, but most days it’s like there’s this gray film over everything. I’m chopping and stirring and making conversation, but I’m not really feeling the enjoyment I used to. I guess I haven’t stopped doing it completely, but inside it feels like I’m just going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling of tiredness and sadness been continuous over the past three years, or have you had periods where you felt better?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty continuous, I think. I’ve had better days here and there, like if something distracts me or the house is busy, but not really a stretch where I felt like myself again.\n\nIt started around… a little over three years ago. Maybe three years and three months now? It was when I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work, mentoring the adjusters and helping oversee those new workflow systems. I remember because everyone kept saying it was a good opportunity, and I was trying to be grateful, but that’s when this tired, sad feeling really settled in and just… stayed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant differences in how you feel at different times of the year?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern with seasons, like winter being worse or summer being better. Sometimes I’ll think, oh, maybe it’s because it’s been gray outside, but then it’ll be sunny and I’ll still feel about the same.\n\nI don’t track it very well, honestly. I get caught up in work and the house and whatever’s going on with everyone else, and before I know it months have passed. So… no, I don’t think there’s a big difference by time of year, at least not one I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms gotten worse over the past three years?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it got sharply worse… it’s more like it settled in and stayed there. It’s been about three years and three months now, since I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work, mentoring the adjusters and helping with those new workflow systems.\n\nAt first I kept thinking, well, this is just a stressful stretch, and you know how offices are when they change systems—everyone’s asking questions, nothing works the way it’s supposed to, and suddenly you’re the person people come to. Like the activity is happening, but the good feeling doesn’t really reach me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I mean… there are moments, I guess. Like if we’re cooking as a family, I can still recognize that it’s supposed to be nice, and my husband will be kind or make some little joke, and I’ll think, “This should feel good.”\n\nBut most days it’s like there’s that gray film over everything. I’m doing it—chopping, stirring, smiling at the right times—but the enjoyment doesn’t really land the way it used to. It’s hard to say I really take pleasure in it, even when I want to.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities where you feel a sense of pleasure or enjoyment that is not diminished?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really, no. I keep trying to think of one, like maybe cooking with everyone, because that used to be such a big thing in our house—my husband would come in and be sweet, or somebody would be laughing about something, and I’d be standing there with the spoon in my hand thinking, this is nice, I should feel nice.\n\nBut most days it’s still like there’s that gray film over everything. I can do it, I can go through the motions and be there with them, but the enjoyment just doesn’t come through the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now feel completely uninterested in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… cooking is probably the main one. It used to be something I actually looked forward to, especially when everybody was around and the kitchen was busy. My husband would come in and be kind, or try to make things feel nice, and I’d usually feel some warmth from that.\n\nNow I still do it, but most days it’s like there’s this gray film over everything. I’m chopping and stirring and making conversation, but I’m not really feeling the enjoyment I used to. I guess I haven’t stopped doing it completely, but inside it feels like I’m just going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling of tiredness and sadness been continuous over the past three years, or have you had periods where you felt better?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty continuous, I think. I’ve had better days here and there, like if something distracts me or the house is busy, but not really a stretch where I felt like myself again.\n\nIt started around… a little over three years ago. Maybe three years and three months now? It was when I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work, mentoring the adjusters and helping oversee those new workflow systems. I remember because everyone kept saying it was a good opportunity, and I was trying to be grateful, but that’s when this tired, sad feeling really settled in and just… stayed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant differences in how you feel at different times of the year?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern with seasons, like winter being worse or summer being better. Sometimes I’ll think, oh, maybe it’s because it’s been gray outside, but then it’ll be sunny and I’ll still feel about the same.\n\nI don’t track it very well, honestly. I get caught up in work and the house and whatever’s going on with everyone else, and before I know it months have passed. So… no, I don’t think there’s a big difference by time of year, at least not one I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms gotten worse over the past three years?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it got sharply worse… it’s more like it settled in and stayed there. It’s been about three years and three months now, since I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work, mentoring the adjusters and helping with those new workflow systems.\n\nAt first I kept thinking, well, this is just a stressful stretch, and you know how offices are when they change systems—everyone’s asking questions, nothing works the way it’s supposed to, and suddenly you’re the person people come to. But the feeling didn’t really lift after that. It’s just been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in how you've been feeling over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Over the past few months… I don’t think there’s been a big change, really. It’s kind of the same thing that’s been going on for about three years and three months now, since I got moved into that higher-responsibility position at work.\n\nThat was when I started mentoring the adjusters and helping oversee those new workflow systems—which, honestly, those systems were supposed to make things easier, but you know how that goes. Everybody has questions, and somehow you become the person who’s expected to know the answer. But anyway… no, the last few months haven’t felt noticeably different. It’s just sort of continued.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods, even if they were brief, where you felt like your old self for at least a couple of months?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might have a decent week here or there, or a weekend where I’m distracted by something, but not a whole couple of months where I’d say I felt like my old self.\n\nIt’s been pretty steady since that work change about three years and three months ago—when they put me in that higher-responsibility role, mentoring the adjusters and dealing with those new workflow systems. And those systems… honestly, they were a whole thing. But no, I can’t point to a couple-month stretch where it really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days recently where you felt significantly better than you have been over the past three years?\n\nPatient: Maybe a day here or there, but nothing I’d call significantly better. It’s more like I get distracted or I’m busy with something and don’t notice it as much.\n\nBut overall, this has really been going on about three years and three months now, since I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work—mentoring the adjusters and overseeing the new workflow systems. Those systems were supposed to streamline things, but of course they just created a whole new set of problems… Anyway, no, not really significantly better.\n\nDoctor: Have you had at least two months in the past three years where you didn't feel tired, sad, or disconnected from activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I really don’t think I’ve had two months like that. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… mostly I came in because I’m just tired all the time. Like, I’ll get to work and by midmorning, even if all I’ve done is review some claim files or walk one of the junior adjusters through something, I feel like my body and my brain are already running on empty. It’s frustrating, because it doesn’t seem like I’ve done enough to feel that worn out.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. It’s not always like crying or anything obvious, it’s more like most days there’s this gray film over everything. Even when my husband’s being sweet, or we’re cooking with the family—which used to be something I really looked forward to—I’m there, I’m doing the chopping or stirring or whatever, but it’s like I’m just going through the motions. \n\nIt reminds me of when the windows in our kitchen get that greasy haze on them and you don’t notice until the sun hits it—sorry, that’s probably not the point. But yes, I do feel down a lot of the time, just kind of muted.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over three years, I think. About three years and three months, if I’m counting right. It started around when they moved me into that higher-responsibility role at work—mentoring the newer adjusters and helping oversee the new workflow systems.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the change, you know, because everyone was confused by the new system and asking me questions all day… but it just never really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I still do the things, like cooking with my family or sitting with my husband when he’s being kind and trying to make things nice, but it’s like I’m not really… there the same way.\n\nMost days it feels like there’s this gray film over everything. I’ll be chopping vegetables or stirring something, and I know I used to enjoy that—especially when everyone was in the kitchen talking over each other—but now I’m mostly just going through the motions. Like the activity is happening, but the good feeling doesn’t really reach me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I mean… there are moments, I guess. Like if we’re cooking as a family, I can still recognize that it’s supposed to be nice, and my husband will be kind or make some little joke, and I’ll think, “This should feel good.”\n\nBut most days it’s like there’s that gray film over everything. I’m doing it—chopping, stirring, smiling at the right times—but the enjoyment doesn’t really land the way it used to. It’s hard to say I really take pleasure in it, even when I want to.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities where you feel a sense of pleasure or enjoyment that is not diminished?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really, no. I keep trying to think of one, like maybe cooking with everyone, because that used to be such a big thing in our house—my husband would come in and be sweet, or somebody would be laughing about something, and I’d be standing there with the spoon in my hand thinking, this is nice, I should feel nice.\n\nBut most days it’s still like there’s that gray film over everything. I can do it, I can go through the motions and be there with them, but the enjoyment just doesn’t come through the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now feel completely uninterested in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… cooking is probably the main one. It used to be something I actually looked forward to, especially when everybody was around and the kitchen was busy. My husband would come in and be kind, or try to make things feel nice, and I’d usually feel some warmth from that.\n\nNow I still do it, but most days it’s like there’s this gray film over everything. I’m chopping and stirring and making conversation, but I’m not really feeling the enjoyment I used to. I guess I haven’t stopped doing it completely, but inside it feels like I’m just going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling of tiredness and sadness been continuous over the past three years, or have you had periods where you felt better?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty continuous, I think. I’ve had better days here and there, like if something distracts me or the house is busy, but not really a stretch where I felt like myself again.\n\nIt started around… a little over three years ago. Maybe three years and three months now? It was when I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work, mentoring the adjusters and helping oversee those new workflow systems. I remember because everyone kept saying it was a good opportunity, and I was trying to be grateful, but that’s when this tired, sad feeling really settled in and just… stayed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant differences in how you feel at different times of the year?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern with seasons, like winter being worse or summer being better. Sometimes I’ll think, oh, maybe it’s because it’s been gray outside, but then it’ll be sunny and I’ll still feel about the same.\n\nI don’t track it very well, honestly. I get caught up in work and the house and whatever’s going on with everyone else, and before I know it months have passed. So… no, I don’t think there’s a big difference by time of year, at least not one I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms gotten worse over the past three years?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it got sharply worse… it’s more like it settled in and stayed there. It’s been about three years and three months now, since I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work, mentoring the adjusters and helping with those new workflow systems.\n\nAt first I kept thinking, well, this is just a stressful stretch, and you know how offices are when they change systems—everyone’s asking questions, nothing works the way it’s supposed to, and suddenly you’re the person people come to. But the feeling didn’t really lift after that. It’s just been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in how you've been feeling over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Over the past few months… I don’t think there’s been a big change, really. It’s kind of the same thing that’s been going on for about three years and three months now, since I got moved into that higher-responsibility position at work.\n\nThat was when I started mentoring the adjusters and helping oversee those new workflow systems—which, honestly, those systems were supposed to make things easier, but you know how that goes. Everybody has questions, and somehow you become the person who’s expected to know the answer. But anyway… no, the last few months haven’t felt noticeably different. It’s just sort of continued.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods, even if they were brief, where you felt like your old self for at least a couple of months?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might have a decent week here or there, or a weekend where I’m distracted by something, but not a whole couple of months where I’d say I felt like my old self.\n\nIt’s been pretty steady since that work change about three years and three months ago—when they put me in that higher-responsibility role, mentoring the adjusters and dealing with those new workflow systems. And those systems… honestly, they were a whole thing. But no, I can’t point to a couple-month stretch where it really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days recently where you felt significantly better than you have been over the past three years?\n\nPatient: Maybe a day here or there, but nothing I’d call significantly better. It’s more like I get distracted or I’m busy with something and don’t notice it as much.\n\nBut overall, this has really been going on about three years and three months now, since I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work—mentoring the adjusters and overseeing the new workflow systems. Those systems were supposed to streamline things, but of course they just created a whole new set of problems… Anyway, no, not really significantly better.\n\nDoctor: Have you had at least two months in the past three years where you didn't feel tired, sad, or disconnected from activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I really don’t think I’ve had two months like that. It’s been… pretty constant, I guess. This started about three years and three months ago, when I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work, mentoring the adjusters and dealing with the new workflow systems.\n\nAnd those systems—everyone said they were going to make things smoother, but then there were all these little issues and people asking me how to fix things… anyway, no, I can’t think of a two-month stretch where I wasn’t feeling that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had at least two months in a row since this started where you didn't feel tired, sad, or disconnected from things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Since it started, it’s just been kind of continuous—about three years and three months now, since I got moved into that higher-responsibility role, mentoring the adjusters and overseeing those new workflow systems.\n\nAnd those systems were supposed to make everything easier, but somehow it felt like I was always the one people came to when something went wrong… but anyway, no, I can’t think of two months in a row where I felt free of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any two-month period since this started where you didn't feel tired or sad at all?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can think of. It’s been about three years and three months now, since I got moved into that higher-responsibility role—mentoring the adjusters and overseeing those new workflow systems. And, you know, those systems were supposed to make things easier, but they just kept pulling me into one thing after another… So no, I don’t think there’s been a two-month period where I didn’t feel that way at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… maybe small stretches where I thought I was handling things a little better, but nothing that really stands out. This has been going on for about three years and three months, ever since I got moved into that higher-responsibility role—mentoring the adjusters and overseeing those new workflow systems.

And that whole change just kind of… it took over everything, you know? The systems were supposed to help, but there was always something with them. So, no, I can’t really point to a solid two weeks where I felt significantly better than I do now.

Like if we’re cooking as a family, I can still recognize that it’s supposed to be nice, and my husband will be kind or make some little joke, and I’ll think, “This should feel good.”\n\nBut most days it’s like there’s that gray film over everything. I’m doing it—chopping, stirring, smiling at the right times—but the enjoyment doesn’t really land the way it used to. I can do it, I can go through the motions and be there with them, but the enjoyment just doesn’t come through the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now feel completely uninterested in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… cooking is probably the main one. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern with seasons, like winter being worse or summer being better. I get caught up in work and the house and whatever’s going on with everyone else, and before I know it months have passed. So… no, I don’t think there’s a big difference by time of year, at least not one I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms gotten worse over the past three years?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it got sharply worse… it’s more like it settled in and stayed there. It’s been about three years and three months now, since I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work, mentoring the adjusters and helping with those new workflow systems.\n\nAt first I kept thinking, well, this is just a stressful stretch, and you know how offices are when they change systems—everyone’s asking questions, nothing works the way it’s supposed to, and suddenly you’re the person people come to. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A little, I guess, but not enough that I feel like myself. Even on days off, I’m still tired. I actually came in because I’m tired all the time—like at work, by midmorning, after just reviewing files or walking one of the junior adjusters through a claim, it feels like my body and my brain are already running on empty.

I’m doing it—chopping, stirring, smiling at the right times—but the enjoyment doesn’t really land the way it used to. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern with seasons, like winter being worse or summer being better. I get caught up in work and the house and whatever’s going on with everyone else, and before I know it months have passed. So… no, I don’t think there’s a big difference by time of year, at least not one I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms gotten worse over the past three years?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it got sharply worse… it’s more like it settled in and stayed there. It’s been about three years and three months now, since I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work, mentoring the adjusters and helping with those new workflow systems.\n\nAt first I kept thinking, well, this is just a stressful stretch, and you know how offices are when they change systems—everyone’s asking questions, nothing works the way it’s supposed to, and suddenly you’re the person people come to.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… mostly I came in because I’m just tired all the time. Like, I’ll get to work and by midmorning, even if all I’ve done is review some claim files or walk one of the junior adjusters through something, I feel like my body and my brain are already running on empty. It’s frustrating, because it doesn’t seem like I’ve done enough to feel that worn out.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. It’s not always like crying or anything obvious, it’s more like most days there’s this gray film over everything. Even when my husband’s being sweet, or we’re cooking with the family—which used to be something I really looked forward to—I’m there, I’m doing the chopping or stirring or whatever, but it’s like I’m just going through the motions. \n\nIt reminds me of when the windows in our kitchen get that greasy haze on them and you don’t notice until the sun hits it—sorry, that’s probably not the point. But yes, I do feel down a lot of the time, just kind of muted.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over three years, I think. About three years and three months, if I’m counting right. It started around when they moved me into that higher-responsibility role at work—mentoring the newer adjusters and helping oversee the new workflow systems.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the change, you know, because everyone was confused by the new system and asking me questions all day… but it just never really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I still do the things, like cooking with my family or sitting with my husband when he’s being kind and trying to make things nice, but it’s like I’m not really… there the same way.\n\nMost days it feels like there’s this gray film over everything. I’ll be chopping vegetables or stirring something, and I know I used to enjoy that—especially when everyone was in the kitchen talking over each other—but now I’m mostly just going through the motions. Like the activity is happening, but the good feeling doesn’t really reach me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I mean… there are moments, I guess. Like if we’re cooking as a family, I can still recognize that it’s supposed to be nice, and my husband will be kind or make some little joke, and I’ll think, “This should feel good.”\n\nBut most days it’s like there’s that gray film over everything. I’m doing it—chopping, stirring, smiling at the right times—but the enjoyment doesn’t really land the way it used to. It’s hard to say I really take pleasure in it, even when I want to.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities where you feel a sense of pleasure or enjoyment that is not diminished?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really, no. I keep trying to think of one, like maybe cooking with everyone, because that used to be such a big thing in our house—my husband would come in and be sweet, or somebody would be laughing about something, and I’d be standing there with the spoon in my hand thinking, this is nice, I should feel nice.\n\nBut most days it’s still like there’s that gray film over everything. I can do it, I can go through the motions and be there with them, but the enjoyment just doesn’t come through the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now feel completely uninterested in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… cooking is probably the main one. It used to be something I actually looked forward to, especially when everybody was around and the kitchen was busy. My husband would come in and be kind, or try to make things feel nice, and I’d usually feel some warmth from that.\n\nNow I still do it, but most days it’s like there’s this gray film over everything. I’m chopping and stirring and making conversation, but I’m not really feeling the enjoyment I used to. I guess I haven’t stopped doing it completely, but inside it feels like I’m just going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling of tiredness and sadness been continuous over the past three years, or have you had periods where you felt better?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty continuous, I think. I’ve had better days here and there, like if something distracts me or the house is busy, but not really a stretch where I felt like myself again.\n\nIt started around… a little over three years ago. Maybe three years and three months now? It was when I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work, mentoring the adjusters and helping oversee those new workflow systems. I remember because everyone kept saying it was a good opportunity, and I was trying to be grateful, but that’s when this tired, sad feeling really settled in and just… stayed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant differences in how you feel at different times of the year?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern with seasons, like winter being worse or summer being better. Sometimes I’ll think, oh, maybe it’s because it’s been gray outside, but then it’ll be sunny and I’ll still feel about the same.\n\nI don’t track it very well, honestly. I get caught up in work and the house and whatever’s going on with everyone else, and before I know it months have passed. So… no, I don’t think there’s a big difference by time of year, at least not one I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms gotten worse over the past three years?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it got sharply worse… it’s more like it settled in and stayed there. It’s been about three years and three months now, since I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work, mentoring the adjusters and helping with those new workflow systems.\n\nAt first I kept thinking, well, this is just a stressful stretch, and you know how offices are when they change systems—everyone’s asking questions, nothing works the way it’s supposed to, and suddenly you’re the person people come to. But the feeling didn’t really lift after that. It’s just been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in how you've been feeling over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Over the past few months… I don’t think there’s been a big change, really. It’s kind of the same thing that’s been going on for about three years and three months now, since I got moved into that higher-responsibility position at work.\n\nThat was when I started mentoring the adjusters and helping oversee those new workflow systems—which, honestly, those systems were supposed to make things easier, but you know how that goes. Everybody has questions, and somehow you become the person who’s expected to know the answer. But anyway… no, the last few months haven’t felt noticeably different. It’s just sort of continued.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods, even if they were brief, where you felt like your old self for at least a couple of months?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might have a decent week here or there, or a weekend where I’m distracted by something, but not a whole couple of months where I’d say I felt like my old self.\n\nIt’s been pretty steady since that work change about three years and three months ago—when they put me in that higher-responsibility role, mentoring the adjusters and dealing with those new workflow systems. And those systems… honestly, they were a whole thing. But no, I can’t point to a couple-month stretch where it really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days recently where you felt significantly better than you have been over the past three years?\n\nPatient: Maybe a day here or there, but nothing I’d call significantly better. It’s more like I get distracted or I’m busy with something and don’t notice it as much.\n\nBut overall, this has really been going on about three years and three months now, since I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work—mentoring the adjusters and overseeing the new workflow systems. Those systems were supposed to streamline things, but of course they just created a whole new set of problems… Anyway, no, not really significantly better.\n\nDoctor: Have you had at least two months in the past three years where you didn't feel tired, sad, or disconnected from activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I really don’t think I’ve had two months like that. It’s been… pretty constant, I guess. This started about three years and three months ago, when I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work, mentoring the adjusters and dealing with the new workflow systems.\n\nAnd those systems—everyone said they were going to make things smoother, but then there were all these little issues and people asking me how to fix things… anyway, no, I can’t think of a two-month stretch where I wasn’t feeling that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had at least two months in a row since this started where you didn't feel tired, sad, or disconnected from things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Since it started, it’s just been kind of continuous—about three years and three months now, since I got moved into that higher-responsibility role, mentoring the adjusters and overseeing those new workflow systems.\n\nAnd those systems were supposed to make everything easier, but somehow it felt like I was always the one people came to when something went wrong… but anyway, no, I can’t think of two months in a row where I felt free of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any two-month period since this started where you didn't feel tired or sad at all?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can think of. It’s been about three years and three months now, since I got moved into that higher-responsibility role—mentoring the adjusters and overseeing those new workflow systems. And, you know, those systems were supposed to make things easier, but they just kept pulling me into one thing after another… So no, I don’t think there’s been a two-month period where I didn’t feel that way at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks where you felt significantly better than you do now?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe small stretches where I thought I was handling things a little better, but nothing that really stands out. I remember because everyone kept saying it was a good opportunity, and I was trying to be grateful, but that’s when this tired, sad feeling really settled in and just… stayed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant differences in how you feel at different times of the year?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern with seasons, like winter being worse or summer being better. Sometimes I’ll think, oh, maybe it’s because it’s been gray outside, but then it’ll be sunny and I’ll still feel about the same.\n\nI don’t track it very well, honestly. I get caught up in work and the house and whatever’s going on with everyone else, and before I know it months have passed. So… no, I don’t think there’s a big difference by time of year, at least not one I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms gotten worse over the past three years?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it got sharply worse… it’s more like it settled in and stayed there. It’s been about three years and three months now, since I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work, mentoring the adjusters and helping with those new workflow systems.\n\nAt first I kept thinking, well, this is just a stressful stretch, and you know how offices are when they change systems—everyone’s asking questions, nothing works the way it’s supposed to, and suddenly you’re the person people come to. But the feeling didn’t really lift after that. It’s just been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in how you've been feeling over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Over the past few months… I don’t think there’s been a big change, really. It’s kind of the same thing that’s been going on for about three years and three months now, since I got moved into that higher-responsibility position at work.\n\nThat was when I started mentoring the adjusters and helping oversee those new workflow systems—which, honestly, those systems were supposed to make things easier, but you know how that goes. Everybody has questions, and somehow you become the person who’s expected to know the answer. But anyway… no, the last few months haven’t felt noticeably different. It’s just sort of continued.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods, even if they were brief, where you felt like your old self for at least a couple of months?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. And those systems… honestly, they were a whole thing. But no, I can’t point to a couple-month stretch where it really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days recently where you felt significantly better than you have been over the past three years?\n\nPatient: Maybe a day here or there, but nothing I’d call significantly better. Those systems were supposed to streamline things, but of course they just created a whole new set of problems… Anyway, no, not really significantly better.\n\nDoctor: Have you had at least two months in the past three years where you didn't feel tired, sad, or disconnected from activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I really don’t think I’ve had two months like that.
